Abstract (英文): Detecting whether and in particular how two data distributions differ is a fundamental statistical problem. For example, we might be interested in which concepts (expressed in natural language) differ between two classes, the outputs of different generative models, or the same model with different prompt specifications. When working with large and complex image datasets, we intuitively think that first selecting concepts from a broad list using fine-grained patch embeddings, and then optimizing the concepts to better fit the data is an effective workflow. However, prior work instead uses MLLMs to propose concepts and image-level embeddings to select them. Therefore, we systematically compare these techniques and find that selecting concepts from broad lists can replace MLLM-proposals at obviously lower cost. MLLM inference can then be spent on optimizing selected concepts instead to achieve substantial improvements. Additionally, by designing a new benchmark that focuses on low-prevalence and multi-concept distinctions, we find that selecting nonredundant concepts instead of only the most distinguishing concepts matters, and we introduce techniques for how to effectively do this. Finally, we show how our methods address important problems like detecting spurious correlations and biases. Overall, our work presents a fundamental perspective shift on how we analyze interpretable distributional differences between datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Robust depth estimation aims to maintain high-quality depths across diverse conditions. However, most existing methods estimate depth without taking into account the object-level information. As a result, the predicted depth may easily deviate within objects and become blurred under adverse conditions. To overcome this weakness, we propose RoSAMDepth, a novel framework that can assist robust self-supervised depth estimation in leveraging rich and diverse object-level priors from the Segment Anything Model (SAM). We focus on incorporating object-level information across three key aspects: a segment-guided representation contrasting method that injects object-level awareness into the feature representation space; an adaptive regional outlier masking strategy combined with a regional Gaussian likelihood loss that enforces regional depth smoothness; and an object-level reliability estimation strategy that mitigates the influence of unreliable supervision. Extensive experiments across multiple datasets and diverse weather conditions demonstrate that our method produces sharper, more accurate depth predictions, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/xagao/RoSAMDepth.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spectroscopic diagnostics provide rich information for characterizing plasma states in tokamak fusion experiments, while existing learning-based approaches are often designed for individual tasks and rely heavily on labeled data. In this work, we propose SpectralTF-MAE, a self-supervised spectral foundation model for tokamak plasma diagnostics. The model is pretrained on paired charge-exchange recombination spectroscopy (CXRS) and X-ray crystal spectroscopy (XCS) measurements through masked spectral reconstruction. To better capture diagnostically important spectral structures, we introduce a peak-aware masking strategy together with a peak-enhanced reconstruction objective, encouraging the model to focus on characteristic spectral regions. A shared Transformer encoder is employed to learn transferable representations across different diagnostic modalities. The pretrained model is further adapted to several downstream tasks, including plasma-parameter prediction, cross-diagnostic spectral generation, and spectral peak analysis. Experiments on EAST diagnostic data demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance across different tasks and consistently benefits from self-supervised spectral pretraining. These results indicate the potential of SpectralTF-MAE as a general and transferable representation learning framework for plasma spectroscopic diagnostics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In the era of large models, stereo matching is developing towards generalizable foundation models trained on million-scale synthetic images. However, how model performance scales up with synthetic data is still underexplored. In this work, we preliminarily investigate the in-domain and cross-domain data scaling laws in stereo matching. A realistic synthetic dataset, named XStereo, containing 200 thousand samples is first built to serve as a high-quality benchmark for data scaling research. In-domain training on five large-scale datasets demonstrate a two-regime scaling behavior with data, transitioning from the standard power law to saturation, where the critical transition point is approximately invariant under a fixed model. Cross-domain performance exhibits a substantially weaker and less predictable dependence on the source-domain data scale. The distribution and coverage diversity are more effective for diminishing domain gaps. More instructive suggestions are provided for high-efficiency data construction and training. The insightful findings are significant verification and extension of the existing scaling laws in both language and vision, paving the way to efficient and stronger stereo matching. The XStereo dataset is public at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Tong-Zhao/XStereo.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in generative image editing enable complex transformations such as background replacement, object manipulation, and pose adjustment from natural language instructions. However, verifying that these transformations preserve visual consistency, defined as the preservation of an object's intrinsic visual attributes, including geometry, texture, color, and fine-grained details, remains a largely unsolved problem. This challenge is particularly pronounced at scale, where manual inspection is infeasible and automated quality assessment (AutoQA) could become essential. In this work, we introduce Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing (V-CAPE), a large-scale dataset for evaluating visual consistency in generative image editing. The dataset consists of 40K unique source-target product image pairs, each annotated with binary accept/reject labels and structured failure modes capturing fine-grained inconsistencies such as geometric distortions, pose errors, and identity mismatches. V-CAPE combines real-world edited images with a synthetic subset constructed from 3D assets, enabling both realistic evaluation and controlled analysis. Vision-language models (VLMs) across all evaluated size categories on AutoQA achieve accuracy below 65%, indicating that the dataset poses a substantial challenge regardless of model scale. Our results highlight fundamental limitations of current models in verifying visual consistency under transformation, positioning V-CAPE as a benchmark for advancing fine-grained image editing evaluation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reliable perception is essential to intelligent transportation systems, yet deep neural networks can silently miss safety-critical objects while continuing to produce apparently plausible outputs. Existing run-time monitors for 3D object detection generally assess each frame independently and therefore cannot exploit the temporal evolution of occlusion, point sparsity, and scene complexity. We present TRIM, a lightweight temporal monitor that detects missed-object failures without modifying or retraining the underlying detector. TRIM summarises intermediate activations using compact channel-wise statistics and processes short sequences of these summaries with a temporal convolutional network. Experiments on NuScenes show that temporal monitoring improves AUROC from 0.686 to 0.704 and reduces the false alarm rate at 95% recall from 0.818 to 0.759 compared with single-frame monitoring. Early and intermediate backbone features provide the strongest monitoring signal, while the temporal inference head requires only 0.159 ms on a CPU.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Jev enables efficient decision-making with large language models (LLMs) through typed querying primitives, allowing the same semantic state to be expressed in different formats, such as binary and multiple-choice queries. However, the consistency of decisions across primitives has not been systematically evaluated. This work introduces PrimaCons, a four-axis benchmark for assessing cross-primitive consistency while preserving the semantics of the query. Evaluations across three Jev sizes reveal consistency violations across all four axes. Our analysis shows that these violations follow systematic patterns and that cross-primitive discrepancies predict decision failure with a ROC-AUC of 0.91. To mitigate these inconsistencies, we propose the Cross-Primitive Residual Calibrator (CPRC), which attaches a lightweight residual network to a frozen backbone and jointly optimizes predictive accuracy and cross-primitive consistency. Under a leave-one-domain-out protocol, CPRC reduces cross-primitive inconsistency by 58.57% and improves accuracy by +2.97%., demonstrating its effectiveness in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings. Our findings establish cross-primitive consistency as a key dimension for evaluating Jev and provide a practical approach to improving the reliability of its decisions. Code and benchmark is available at https://github.com/67L1/PrimaCons-CRPR.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume that the information required to answer a query is complete, leaving models to only wait or answer. In the real world, however, information is often incomplete due to uncertainty from either the video or the user. In proactive paradigms, such uncertainty can cause the model to wait forever or generate highly hallucinatory responses. We therefore argue that proactive interaction should be redefined with a third action: ask, allowing models to actively ask for missing information. To study when and what models should ask before answering, we introduce AskStream, the first multi-turn benchmark spanning video and user uncertainty for streaming video understanding, with four task categories and eight subtasks. It contains 438 videos with 2,714 manually annotated frame-level time windows across three types: wait, ask, and answer. Across both multiple-choice and open-ended settings, even the strongest model attains only 52.18% on our proposed Ask-F1 metric, highlighting substantial limitations in proactive information seeking under uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention over packed geometric data. We introduce PACK3D, a local-global attention operator that separates memory layout from attention geometry. PACK3D keeps local neighborhood attention exact, compresses only distant context into contiguous 3D block summaries, and indexes every query-key interaction with axial RoPE on true token-grid coordinates rather than packed positions. Because the rotation is applied before a block is summarized, the query-to-summary logit remains a weighted sum of query-relative 3D displacements; compressed global context therefore stays spatially interpretable after packing, instead of collapsing to anonymous pooled content. We measure the failure mode directly. On a controlled synthetic task---train on one packing, change only the memory order of the tokens, hold the physical labeling rule fixed---learned positional embeddings and packed-sequence RoPE solve the training layout perfectly yet collapse under Morton, block-contiguous, and random repacking, while axial grid-coordinate RoPE stays exact throughout. The same coordinate system carries to real data: on Allen WTC-11 hiPSC microscopy, PACK3D improves over a parameter-matched 3D U-Net on the 15-structure joint task and over a matched learned-position local-global baseline, with controls isolating the coordinate system, not encoder capacity, as the source of the gain. A fused Triton implementation matches windowed-attention latency at 65k tokens while supplying the long-range context windowing cannot---so the operator is not only correct but trainable at scale.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human handwriting is produced as an ordered sequence of pen movements, but an offline image keeps only the final ink. Handwriting trajectory recovery aims to restore the ordered stroke points and their pen-up/pen-down transitions. Existing image-trajectory pair datasets are recorded with digitizing tablets under controlled conditions, which limits writer coverage and stylistic diversity. Moreover, there is no benchmark that measures the visual grounding capabilities of general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for the stroke trajectory recovery task. In this work, we introduce inkGround, a benchmark for stroke trajectory recovery from full-page, camera-captured handwriting. inkGround pairs 537 page images (~18.7k words, ~163k strokes) with reference trajectories produced by a pretrained offline-to-online model, filtered by OCR agreement, and verified by human raters. Because these references are pseudo-labels that represent one plausible pen trajectory, we also evaluate VLMs on the IAM-OnDB dataset, which provides recorded pen trajectories. We cast the task as text-conditioned point grounding: given a page image and a target word, the model must locate the word and output its ordered stroke points. Outputs are scored by trajectory fidelity (DTW, Chamfer distance), readability of the rendered strokes (Accurate Rate, Correct Rate), and style similarity. We benchmark four open-source and three frontier closed-source VLMs with a shared in-context prompt. Closed-source models, notably Gemini-3.1-Pro, produce legible trajectories close to the reference, and this ranking also holds on IAM-OnDB.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual rule reasoning asks whether multimodal models can recover an abstract concept from a few examples. Existing studies either infer rules from visual examples but evaluate only classification or verbalization, or supervise executable generation with rules provided as input. Consequently, the relationship between visual rule understanding and reliable rule instantiation remains insufficiently explored. We present Bongard-Forge100k, a controlled benchmark, together with RuleForge, a backbone-agnostic framework that jointly learns visual rule understanding and rule-conditioned executable generation. The benchmark contains 108,797 problems: 86,000 for training, 19,297 for testing familiar rules, and 3,500 for testing held-out rule compositions. The framework progresses through rule-understanding training, joint understanding and scene-program supervision, and refinement via execution-grounded reinforcement learning with Understanding Replay. Executing generated programs under multiple random seeds provides verifiable feedback on executability, rule satisfaction, diversity, and robustness. With UniLIP as the primary backbone, RuleForge improves multiple-choice accuracy over the base model by 20.93 percentage points on familiar rules and 28.42 percentage points on held-out rule compositions, while increasing scene-level rule satisfaction on familiar rules from 2.91% to 70.99%. Additional experiments on InternVL3.5, Qwen3-VL, and Show-o2 demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed training data and framework across diverse multimodal architectures.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The scale of modern foundation models enables broad task generalization. In medical imaging, where segmentation demands vary widely across clinical contexts, such flexibility is invaluable. But current models, such as SAM trade adaptability for scale, locking their utility behind high-end infrastructure and leaving behind the low-resource environments most in need of diagnostic support. To bridge this gap, we introduce Foundational-NCA, a lightweight foundation model capable of adapting to a wide range of medical segmentation tasks without retraining. Built on Neural Cellular Automata (NCA), Foundational-NCA incorporates gradient-based learning with genetic optimization over a latent task space, using a compact task vector to guide predictions. This enables dynamic adaptation via a genetic prompt mechanism, allowing the model to generalize across tasks while keeping the parameter count magnitudes lower at just 211 thousand, compared to SAM-based models with 38.9 to 636 million parameters. We evaluate Foundational-NCA on 15 trained and 6 entirely unseen segmentation tasks from the TotalSegmentator dataset, achieving an average Dice score of 84.5% on known tasks and 60.4% on unseen ones. These results demonstrate that task generalization is possible in ultra-compact models, laying the groundwork for resource-efficient, deployable foundation models in clinical environments where infrastructure is limited. By reducing model size by over 180 times, Foundational-NCA takes a step toward a more equitable future for medical AI, one where powerful tools are within reach of every healthcare setting.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reliable vocal-cord localization in emergency video laryngoscopy remains visually challenging due to rapid tissue deformation, specular highlights, and instrument occlusions. We propose Local Point Tracking (LPT), a robust framework that combines a single-frame detector, local point propagation, and stateless mask refinement. LPT follows a localize first, snap second pipeline. First, a single-frame semantic detector generates sparse anchors, within which LPT initializes local query points (SAM-supported by default, with a regular grid as fallback). A point-propagation module tracks these points through detector-missed frames and recovers temporal bounding boxes from their geometry. Second, because point-derived boxes can be loose around the visible boundary, a box-snap step refines the selected box to the target boundary. This decomposition keeps temporal state in local point coordinates rather than a stateful mask or whole-region representation, while the spatial refinement module handles single-frame boundary precision without feeding mask errors back into temporal memory. Extensive evaluations on two distinct datasets demonstrate that our proposed LPT method achieves strong overall performance against existing baselines, with the best mAP_50, success AUC, and missing rate on both datasets. Furthermore, ablation studies confirm that point tracking primarily enhances temporal tracking robustness, while box-snap refinement effectively improves boundary tightness.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly process multiple views of the same scene, but it remains unclear whether they integrate these observations into a coherent scene-level representation. Existing multi-image evaluations do not isolate this capability, as tasks may admit correct solutions through independent per-view processing without requiring cross-view correspondence. We introduce OneScene-Bench, a controlled benchmark designed to explicitly distinguish when cross-view integration is necessary. Our benchmark evaluates three complementary capabilities on real-world multi-view scenes: unique object counting, cross-view consistency, and spatial reasoning. For counting, we control object overlap across views to separate cases solvable by simple per-view aggregation from those requiring instance-level correspondence. For consistency, we introduce localized edits in shared and view-specific regions, testing whether models distinguish genuine scene contradictions from differences caused by viewpoint and visibility. For spatial reasoning, we construct object relations that require combining spatial evidence across viewpoints to compare physical proximity. We evaluate twelve state-of-the-art open-weight and proprietary VLMs and find that counting accuracy drops substantially on complementary views, ranging from 4.5-50.0%, compared with 45.5-81.5% on complete-overlap examples. Moreover, cross-view consistency remains around 50% across models, including recent proprietary VLMs, despite substantially stronger performance on spatial reasoning. Our results reveal a substantial gap between processing multiple images and integrating evidence across them, highlighting the need for evaluations that explicitly test scene-level multi-view understanding. Code and data are available at https://github.com/swathisuhas/OneScene-Bench.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting predicts a scene from a few images, but our predictor is less accurate than its own Gaussians optimized per scene on those images. This gap lies mostly in the shape of the Gaussians, their scale and rotation, since optimizing the shape alone recovers almost all of it and opacity and color recover less. Gradient steps on the input views correct this shape, and the model holds these views at inference. Log-anisotropy correction changes the axis ratios of each Gaussian in log space at constant volume, and in-forward gradient refinement renders the Gaussians into the input views and applies the gradient of the photometric loss with a learned recurrent update, first to opacity and color and then to the shape. GradSplat is a feed-forward model that refines its own Gaussians by learned gradient steps inside its forward pass, with no target view. On both complete six-view RealEstate10K protocols, it has the best PSNR and SSIM among nine methods and keeps its accuracy from $6$ to $42$ input views. Without fine-tuning, it is the most accurate on DL3DV and has the best PSNR and SSIM on ScanNet++. The code will be released at https://github.com/Jingong-Chen/GradSplat/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to significant progress in visual reasoning capabilities. However, existing benchmarks curate image-question pairs pertaining to object categories with few instances, enabling trivial object referencing and limited visual ambiguity. In this work, we introduce MIMO-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in a Multi-Instance per Multi-Object categories (MIMO) setting. Each image-question pair in our benchmark pertains to instances belonging to object categories with multiple instances. The presence of such similar feature distractors causes hindrance in basic visual reasoning tasks, necessitating the referring expression to be composed of multiple visual subtasks to uniquely and unambiguously identify the target object. MIMO-Bench comprises 3,802 image-question pairs across attribute binding and numerical counting tasks. Despite impressive performances on existing benchmarks, both SOTA open-source (InternVL, QwenVL) and proprietary (Gemini, GPT-5) models exhibit substantially lower performance on MIMO-Bench, highlighting their limitations in the MIMO setting. The benchmark will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Severe blur, downsampling, noise, and compression can erase the identity cues needed to faithfully restore a person's face. We therefore use photographs of the same person as an external source of identity. AlbumVSR conditions a frozen, one-step streaming diffusion transformer on a personal album. We use four MagFace/CLIP set tokens to encode the album, and 64 texture tokens which come from a fused UV atlas rendered at the current window's estimated face pose. These tokens are appended to the transformer's cached cross-attention context. On an identity-disjoint VoxCeleb2 benchmark with heavy synthetic degradation, AlbumVSR raises ArcFace similarity to the ground truth from 0.586 to 0.670 and face PSNR from 24.98 to 26.71 dB. On the 91 test identities, its mean identity similarity is higher than that of all 14 baselines.

Abstract (英文): Images produced by generative models appear strikingly similar to real ones. In this work, we study whether perfection has been reached by generated content in terms of matching the properties of real data. We train deep neural networks to separate real data from fake and observe their test accuracies. Only a chance performance has any scope of proving statistical indistinguishability. However, since generative models are known to sometimes introduce small errors, e.g., low level checkerboard artifacts, we create progressive abstractions of the original real/fake images to potentially bring them to a space where those artifacts get wiped away. These include operations such as blurring, downsizing, cropping, converting RGB images into edge/semantic maps, etc. To our surprise, almost none of the abstractions are able to make fake data indistinguishable from real. The only cases when neural network based classifiers fail to separate the two categories is when the data is abstracted to such an extent that the original content seems mostly lost to the human eye, e.g., downsizing a $256 \times 256$ RGB image of human face to $4 \times 4$ resolution. We observe this trend across four different datasets of varying complexity (MNIST, CelebA, FFHQ and GenImage) and across various types of generative models (GANs, VAEs, diffusion models). This shows that the nature of fake artifacts extends all the way from low level to very high level in images. The simple implication is that fake images are much more different from real than we are used to think.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent generation methods produce 3D assets with detailed geometry and rich textures. However, these assets are non-emissive: they reflect light but emit none. We study a new task, emission generation: predicting spatially varying RGB emission over a 3D asset's surface from its geometry, non-emissive materials, and a rendered view.
  To support this task, we introduce LightGenBench. Our benchmark makes three contributions. First, we curate a dataset of 37k artist-authored emissive assets in UV, voxel, and multi-view form. Second, we establish baselines across four method families: UV-domain, sparse-voxel, multi-view, and segmentation-based generation. Third, we design an evaluation protocol that measures where an asset emits and how, complemented by user-study comparisons on renderings with varying emission strength.
  Across the four families, the sparse-voxel baseline TRELLIS.2-Emission performs best because generating emission natively in 3D avoids the failure modes of the other representations, such as the fracturing artifacts of UV space and the occluded emitters that multi-view generation cannot see. However, TRELLIS.2-Emission only reaches an IoU of 0.271, leaving the task far from solved. We hope LightGenBench establishes emission generation as a benchmarkable problem for 3D generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 4D perception models, which classify 3D point-cloud videos, are being built into robots, assistive monitoring and AR, yet before deploying one a practitioner cannot check which cue it relies on: how a body part moves, or what shape it has. 3D attribution is single-frame, video attribution is confined to RGB pixels, gradient maps need white-box access, attention is absent from state-space backbones, and point-level perturbations assume a point correspondence across frames that real depth and LiDAR data lack. We present Kinematic Causal Attribution (KCA), a black-box, interventional attribution method that queries only the model's outputs. Its freeze operator removes a region's motion while keeping its shape, and a shape-free proxy removes the shape while keeping the motion. Together they span a motion$\times$geometry cube for every tracked region, whose order-symmetric Shapley decomposition is complete and whose interaction is measured rather than assumed away. The operators rewrite per-frame point sets, so they need no correspondences, and a context-averaged estimator handles structures that span several regions. We validate KCA on the official PSTNet, P4Transformer and Mamba4D trained on MSR-Action3D. The decisive test is an audit: two binary tasks are built from real clips whose classes differ only in the motion or only in the shape of the same arm, and the motion share of the arm's importance follows the defining cue on all three backbones (0.83 vs. 0.60 on P4Transformer, 0.86 vs. 0.30 on PSTNet, 0.92 vs. 0.61 on Mamba4D), a distinction no single attribution map can make. The split costs no faithfulness: the necessity maps have the lowest deletion AUC on P4Transformer and PSTNet, tie with the best baseline on Mamba4D, and lose their correlation with the trained model's once the weights are randomised. Skeleton ground truth, re-sampling stability and cost complete the validation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spatial transcriptomics (ST) enables joint characterization of tissue morphology and gene expression. However, 3D volumetric ST acquisition remains expensive. AI-driven methods offer a low-cost alternative but remain limited in accuracy, while recent 2D section-wise ST acquisition still rely on dense grid sampling, resulting in redundant measurements and undersampling of biologically informative regions. To bridge the gap, we propose STRATA, a reinforcement-driven active sampling framework with tri-paradigm adaptation for reconstructing dense 3D transcriptomic maps from sparse voxel-wise ST acquisition. STRATA constructs a 3D graph integrating H&E morphology with single-cell priors and employs a reinforcement learning agent to estimate morphological sparsity and transcriptomic informativeness, enabling budget-aware selection of informative spots. Given these sparse measurements, an adaptive tri-paradigm predictor combines retrieval, regression, and cross-layer imputation through lightweight fusion, dynamically weighting their contributions. Experiments across datasets demonstrate improved sampling efficiency and accuracy, particularly under low-budget settings. Code is available at https://github.com/Junchao-Zhu/STRATA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The central bottleneck in visual generation and editing has migrated from achieving pixel-level fidelity to satisfying densely constrained user specifications through increasingly agentic workflows. Prevailing agentic generation pipelines remain shallow generate-verify-rewrite loops treating VLM judgments as coarse visual feedback, either discarding repair experience or admitting ungated updates that poison skill library. With memorize and govern periods left open, they degrade into ungoverned iterators of repeated trial-and-error rather than continual self-evolving harness. Towards this issue, we present \textbf{VEvo-Harness}, the first causally-guarded continual self-evolving harness system for agentic visual generation. VEvo-Harness introduces four coupled innovations: (1) \textit{a visual traceback} that densifies sparse judgments into root-cause attributions with a hierarchical verification tree; (2) \textit{a visual continual governance} that admits only causally valid skill patches through counterfactual dual-arm image generation replay; (3) \textit{a lifecycle-managed harness state} that hierarchically distills root-cause-anchored episodes across working, experience, and pattern tiers into a persistent skill library of routable and causally verified capabilities; (4) \textit{a heterogeneous sandbox policy} that orchestrates a diverse action space of repair primitives, dynamically routing cost-aware interventions based on root causes to select the most effective strategy. VEvo-Harness surpasses state-of-the-art agentic methods and rivals proprietary generators (\textit{e.g.}, GPT-Image, Nano-Pro) while maintaining the continual harness evolution and generalization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): With the rapid advancement of text-to-image diffusion models, increasingly realistic AI-generated content has raised serious concerns about misuse and copyright infringement. Digital watermarking offers a promising solution by enabling user-level traceability, yet existing methods remain difficult to deploy at scale, hindered by costly model retraining or detection, poor effectiveness-fidelity trade-offs, and limited robustness to image transformations. To address these challenges, we propose CLaW (Codec-guided Latent Watermarking), a robust and efficient watermarking framework for traceable diffusion image generation with frozen diffusion backbones and inversion-free detection. Specifically, CLaW first uses a pretrained watermark codec to map each watermark message to a latent residual, enabling low-cost sampling-time injection. Then, the residual is injected within a late denoising window, where image semantics are better preserved and the watermark signal is less perturbed by subsequent denoising updates, yielding a better balance between watermark effectiveness and image fidelity. Furthermore, we introduce a decoder-guided adaptive injection mechanism that uses decoding confidence as feedback to dynamically calibrate watermark strength, reinforcing weak watermark signals and improving robustness under image transformations. Extensive experiments show that CLaW preserves high visual fidelity and achieves state-of-the-art robustness under image transformations, enabling reliable user-level traceability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aligning a partial, real-world 3D face scan to a canonical representation requires solving two coupled problems at once: estimating the rigid pose that brings the scan into a common frame, and completing the facial shape in regions the scan never observed. Existing pipelines typically decouple these steps, running a generic point-cloud registration front end followed by a separate non-rigid fitting or deformation stage. Building on the GeoTransformer superpoint-matching architecture, we propose a single network that predicts both a rigid pose and a shape-complete point cloud in one forward pass: a cross-attention regressor, conditioned on transformer point features and predicted coarse correspondences, maps a partial scan to per-patch coefficients of a 3D morphable face model learned offline from a corpus of 8,000 identities. The resulting morphed template is then used, in the same forward pass, as the target for fine-grained Sinkhorn matching and rigid pose estimation, supervised purely by geometric alignment to ground-truth morphed geometry, so no hand-labeled coefficients are required. Against six classical and learned non-rigid registration baselines on held-out identities, our method leads the strongest ones by 3-4 times on per-vertex error and baselines that collapse the head onto the visible region by more than an order of magnitude, and is simultaneously the most accurate and fastest method on ground-truth-free scans from two independent real-scan datasets, NoW and NPHM. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Edited endpoint images make the target of an image-to-video transition explicit and can improve transition completion, but also preserve context in the source image's layout. Full-frame endpoint conditioning turns this preserved context into requirements on the video's final state, potentially inducing temporal artifacts. We measure discontinuity with Hard-Cut (HC), late background reversal with Background Motion Reversal (BMR), and motion suppression with Dynamic Degree (DD). These artifacts appear across five video models spanning training-free and learned endpoint conditioning, in model-specific forms. We study Partial, a selective conditioning scheme that constrains only an edit-relevant region estimated from image-editor attention and leaves the remaining terminal content to the video model. Across three backbones on TC-Bench-I2V, Partial reduces temporal artifacts relative to full-frame conditioning, with no significant difference in Transition Completion Ratio (TCR); on Wan2.2-Turbo, uniformly weakening conditioning does not reproduce these reductions. A donor-context intervention keeps full-frame conditioning and replaces only the context outside the edit-relevant region with the final frame of a Partial video. It lowers HC and BMR on HunyuanVideo-1.5 and lowers BMR while restoring DD on Wan2.2-Turbo, with no significant difference in TCR, so constraint extent alone does not account for these artifacts. Together, these findings suggest that edited endpoints should serve as partial future goals, although edit relevance does not guarantee future validity. Code and data will be released at https://github.com/wannagetacat/Edited-Endpoints-I2V.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Underwater image enhancement aims to correct color and recover detail degraded by wavelength-dependent attenuation, scattering, and noise. Paired-reference supervision constrains output similarity, but does not by itself specify the color mapping, the strength of local detail corrections, or consistency with the degraded observation. We propose MCL-UIE, a paired-supervised framework that addresses these requirements by constraining restoration operations and the training objective. Monotonic Chromatic Transform (MCT) restricts global color mapping in log-LMS space through positive diagonal entries and bounded cross-channel coefficients. Uncertainty-Gated Wavelet Reconstruction (UGWR) modulates high-frequency residuals with a learned confidence gate calibrated against normalized wavelet reconstruction errors. During training, Reference-Anchored Degradation Replay (RADR) renders the enhanced image and its paired reference using shared latent degradation proxies, encouraging both to reproduce the input. The three components share a restoration network that combines structured color correction, locally controlled detail recovery, and reference-anchored replay. MCL-UIE ranks first in thirteen of sixteen full-reference dataset-metric comparisons and produces competitive visual results on no-reference benchmarks. Objective ablations indicate that confidence calibration is most effective when combined with replay supervision. RADR is used only during training and adds no inference-time computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/Wangtao-Bao/MCL-UIE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is increasingly explored as an input representation for 3D perception. Yet it remains unclear how current 3D architectures exploit heterogeneous Gaussian attributes. This ambiguity makes accuracy hard to interpret and limits principled architecture design for 3DGS perception. However, existing object-level 3DGS datasets lack the appearance variation or artifact control needed for systematic attribute-level analysis. We introduce COGS, a large-scale 3DGS dataset with 15,675 artifact-filtered and appearance-rich objects across 40 categories. Built on COGS, we establish COGS-Bench for object classification and attribute-usage diagnosis, complemented by external validation. Our benchmark shows that Gaussian attributes are not uniformly beneficial: selected subsets can outperform the full attribute set, and the effects of appearance, opacity, and covariance-related attribute combinations vary substantially across architectures. We further study this finding with HALO, a reference model that preserves attribute-specific evidence before hierarchical aggregation. Experiments on COGS-Bench and external 3DGS datasets demonstrate that COGS supports systematic diagnosis of 3DGS attribute usage, while HALO highlights the value of role-preserving attribute organization. Our dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/XiaoZhengCV/COGS.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Embodied world models should not only imagine plausible futures, but also perceive the geometric, semantic, and motion structure of the evolving world. We present GenSight, a unified embodied world model that brings future video generation and dense perception into a single pretrained video diffusion transformer. GenSight uses a unified spatiotemporal representation for video, depth, embodiment segmentation, and first-frame-relative 2D flow. Built on a shared DiT backbone, GenSight uses lightweight task modulation to predict all modalities within the same architecture. All tasks are jointly optimized within a unified velocity prediction space. At inference, GenSight supports two complementary modes: multi-step future video generation with aligned dense predictions and single-step dense perception from an observed video. By unifying modality representation, model architecture, and prediction space, GenSight avoids separate modality-specific pipelines and couples appearance, geometry, semantics, and motion in a shared representation. It promotes cross-modal consistency and allows dense perception to benefit from the spatiotemporal priors learned through video generation. Trained on embodied manipulation data spanning multiple robot embodiments and human-hand interactions, GenSight demonstrates strong performance in both video generation and dense 4D perception, highlighting the effectiveness of unifying seeing and imagining for embodied world modeling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-image composition (MICo) is a critical yet challenging capability for large-scale image editing models, where consistency degrades rapidly as the number of reference images increases. While supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can equip models with basic MICo ability, it provides only implicit supervision on the composed image and often fails to learn reliable object-level correspondence across multiple references. We propose DeCoRL, a reinforcement-learning (RL) framework that improves MICo consistency by decomposing holistic MICo consistency into object-level consistency. DeCoRL first establishes instruction-relevant object correspondences across reference images and localizes the corresponding regions in the generated image. It then uses a VLM-based reward model trained on single-object consistency annotations to produce fine-grained, object-wise feedback, which is aggregated into a multi-object consistency reward to guide RL post-training. We further introduce DeCo-Bench, a benchmark covering compositions from 2 to 6 reference images. Experiments on DeCo-Bench and the public MICo-Bench demonstrate consistent improvements in MICo consistency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Continuous Conditional Generative Modeling (CCGM) aims to synthesize high-dimensional data conditioned on continuously valued scalar labels. However, existing continuous diffusion models face severe data sparsity under imbalanced and long-tailed label distributions, which can lead to visual artifacts and condition inconsistency. Moreover, inference-time classifier-free guidance and dense pixel-space integration introduce substantial sampling overhead, limiting practical deployment. To address these challenges, we propose CAViLD, an efficient latent diffusion framework for continuous conditional generation. CAViLD first constructs a purified condition-aware latent space through density-adaptive vicinal learning, auxiliary semantic regularization, and NIQE-guided negative data augmentation. It then internalizes the conditional guidance direction through an EMA teacher, enabling single-branch conditional inference, and introduces covariance-aware progressive distillation for label-dependent probability-flow ODEs, supporting sampling down to four NFEs. Experiments on RC-49, UTKFace, and Steering Angle demonstrate a favorable trade-off among generation fidelity, continuous condition consistency, and sampling efficiency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page: https://worldflow-rbs.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Online vectorized map construction provides autonomous vehicles with essential, real-time semantic and geometric scene understanding. While leveraging prior maps presents an opportunity to improve accuracy, they are often biased due to outdated information or temporary road changes. Existing methods, which typically treat priors as fully reliable or only consider limited noise patterns, suffer severe performance degradation when these priors are imperfect. To address this, we propose PriorMaskMap, a robust framework for prior-guided vectorized map construction. Its first innovation is a Prior-Aware Confidence Estimator (PACE) that evaluates the input rasterized map against current visual observations. It assigns confidence scores to regions classified as reliable, erroneous, or blank, creating a foundational mask that guides two subsequent branches. In one branch, the Dual-Prior Query Generator (DPQG) selectively filters and embeds both rasterized and vectorized priors to initialize informative queries from trustworthy map structures. In the other, the Confidence-Guided Prior Fusion (CGPF) module spatially propagates and refines the mask, using the refined version to govern the fusion of BEV features with the prior map, thereby providing rich contextual representations for the map decoder. Evaluation on the nuScenes dataset shows that PriorMaskMap achieves superior mapping precision over existing approaches and exhibits marked robustness when provided with priors containing apparent errors. Our code is released at https://github.com/healenrens/PriorMaskMap.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autoregressive (AR) image generators are powerful in principle but inflexible in practice: training with a fixed raster-scan order hardcodes a single factorization of the image distribution into the model, leaving it unable to inpaint, outpaint, or edit at inference time. Existing solutions, including the modern discrete-diffusion family, train from scratch on a mixture of token orderings, paying roughly 3x the compute of standard raster-scan training; discrete diffusion further sacrifices compatibility with the AR inference ecosystem (KV caching, vLLM) by construction. We argue that this cost is unnecessary. A pretrained raster-scan AR model has already learned the joint distribution over image tokens; what it lacks is not knowledge but the ability to express that knowledge through non-sequential conditional pathways. We propose a recipe for adapting pretrained raster-scan models into any-order generators using two components: future-aware positional embeddings that resolve a target-position identifiability problem at negligible overhead, and an ordering curriculum that traces a stable optimization path from the pretrained solution to a good any-order basin. Applied to four base models across two architectures and two scales (LlamaGen-L/XL, RAR-L/XL), the recipe substantially outperforms MaskGIT on ImageNet inpainting (FID 6.21 vs. 10.44) and outpainting, is competitive with MAR-L, trades a 1.2-1.9 increase in class-conditional FID for this flexibility, and costs 13-33% additional compute beyond pretraining, while leaving the standard AR inference stack untouched. Inpainting, outpainting, and editing, our results suggest, need not be added to autoregressive image models. They can be unlocked from the ones that exist. Code is available at https://github.com/Ordered-AR/unlockedadaptation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present Youtu-Parsing-Omni, an omni-modal parser that converts documents, images, audio, and video into structured records grounded in space and time, providing a perception layer for agents that read, locate, and cite evidence across modalities. Its design rests on one encoder for every modality and one schema for every task. Youtu-Omni-Encoder, initialized from a pre-trained language model to strengthen text reading, encodes all modalities with shared weights in a unified space-time coordinate system, and a few fusion layers let video frames attend to concurrent audio, bringing cross-modal fusion and temporal alignment into perception itself. OmniSchema unifies the outputs of all ten parsing tasks, allowing a single self-evolving post-training procedure, OmniSchema-Aware On-Policy Distillation (OSAD), to provide a shared token-role-based objective across tasks. In each round, the model is trained on its own rollouts against token-level targets from a reference-conditioned copy of itself, which is then refreshed with the trained weights, so that the parser and its supervision co-evolve. With a single 5 B-parameter checkpoint, Youtu-Parsing-Omni achieves state-of-the-art performance on OmniDocBench v1.6 (96.96 Overall) and leads all open-weight models on OmniParsingBench (75.08 Avg.), with its advantage concentrated on audio and video. It also achieves the best overall chart-parsing score (95.02) and the highest exact chemical-fingerprint agreement among compared systems, and matches a dedicated optical-music model in character-level transcription of typeset sheet music while staying far ahead of general-purpose systems. Code and model weights are publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generating editable computer-aided design (CAD) models from text requires selecting operations that produce the intended changes to the geometry constructed so far. An incorrect operation can compromise subsequent steps, making reliable multi-step generation challenging. Existing methods based on large language models (LLMs) primarily learn to predict commands, without explicit supervision of the geometric states those commands should produce. Inspired by world-action models, we introduce CAD-WAM, which couples geometry-conditioned action generation with next-state prediction. We define complete modeling operations as actions in a CAD domain-specific language (DSL) and encode surface point clouds of intermediate solids into compact latent states using a pretrained variational autoencoder. An LLM backbone generates the next action from the target description and current geometric state, while an auxiliary head on the same backbone predicts the next state to provide geometric supervision during training. We construct step-wise training trajectories from parametric CAD models in the DeepCAD dataset, pairing each trajectory with an expert-level textual description. At inference, each generated action is executed by a CAD kernel, and the resulting solid is re-encoded to inform the next decision. On the DeepCAD test set, CAD-WAM outperforms the task-specific baselines in geometric accuracy and output validity, with larger gains on models requiring more operations, and produces more accurate geometry than general-purpose LLMs prompted with the same DSL. Ablations further show that next-state supervision reduces mean Chamfer distance by 13.2\% relative to an otherwise identical model trained only to predict actions. Code is available at https://github.com/Lzy-1037/CAD-WAM.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal videos contain rich temporal cues that can facilitate faithful fusion. However, existing video fusion methods mainly rely on short-range temporal interactions, leaving valuable long-term information underexplored. Moreover, inconsistent fusion representations can cause flickering artifacts, undermining temporal coherence. To address these coupled challenges, we propose MemoryFusion, a two-stage framework integrating cross-temporal memory learning with progressive temporal refinement. Considering the complementary roles of adjacent and distant frames, the first stage combines a Short-Term Memory Bank for local temporal guidance with a Long-Term Memory Bank that preserves reliable historical information through a fusion-oriented update strategy. To mitigate temporal fluctuations, a Temporal Smoothing Module leverages soft motion cues for coarse background stabilization. Building upon the resulting fusion prior, The second stage further employs a lightweight 3D residual Temporal Refinement Module to correct remaining temporal inconsistencies while enhancing structural details. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MemoryFusion achieves superior fusion quality and temporal consistency over existing frame-based and video-based fusion methods. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/Meiqi-Gong/MemoryFusion.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal tool-using agents augment vision-language models with access to external information and capabilities, enabling them to solve knowledge-intensive and visually complex tasks that cannot be addressed using visual context alone. However, existing reinforcement learning pipelines largely train single-role agents to search and answer using terminal rewards, overlooking how humans inspect retrieved evidence, reformulate queries, and revise conclusions during search. Consequently, they fail to optimize this self-corrective process over complete interaction trajectories. We propose MRC-MMAgent, a multi-role collaborative reinforcement learning framework that incorporates trajectory assessment and continuation directly into the executable agent loop. First, the framework establishes a multi-role collaborative loop that integrates solving, inspection, and continuation, enabling the agent to acquire evidence through tools, evaluate the preceding trajectory, and resume problem solving when the current answer is insufficiently supported. Second, we introduce a role-coupled learning objective that combines task success, interaction validity, and successful correction with full-trajectory policy optimization across the decisions generated by all roles. Extensive experiments show that, using only the original baseline training set without constructing additional datasets, our method consistently outperforms the corresponding base models and achieves leading or highly competitive performance across benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual commissions require image agents to integrate heterogeneous source materials, preserve requirements across dependent stages, and produce coordinated deliverables. Existing evaluations examine specific capabilities and execution settings, leaving end-to-end commission completion less understood. We introduce Visual Commission Bench (VCB), comprising 600 tasks across 12 categories and three complementary tiers, from capability-focused tasks to complete commissions. VCB uses 5,039 requirement checks and separate visual quality ratings to assess content correctness, visual realization, constraint preservation, and delivery completeness without prescribing an internal workflow. We present V-Protocol, a reference agent that dynamically composes reusable task, cognitive, and expression modules at inference time. Task modules organize delivery, cognitive modules derive content, and expression modules guide visual realization. The modules exchange structured intermediate artifacts, while a shared execution harness tracks artifact revisions and dependencies, manages tool execution, and checks completion. With the same controller and image generator as the agent baselines, V-Protocol achieves 69.0\% strict task success on VCB, compared with 42.0\% for SCOPE. On Mind-Bench, strict accuracy increases from 39.2\% to 45.4\%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly prompted with personas, such as "You have low vision", to stand in for people in user studies. We ask whether a persona makes a model see like the person it describes. We compare persona answers with measured people with low vision, color blindness or hearing loss, and with simple rules that use only the facts the persona states. The persona never beats these rules, and it erases the real differences between groups and individuals. Describing blur leaves recognition unchanged in all 14 models we test, while blurring the photo lowers it as it does for people. Inside open models, the description changes neither the image information nor the answer probabilities. Yet the models know the effects they fail to show: they can estimate how blur and illusions affect people. Telling is not seeing: a persona makes a model say what it knows about people, not see what they see.

</details>

Abstract (英文): With the growing success of diffusion models in computer vision, we explore their potential for same-identity facial replacement in photographs. Specifically, we propose a diffusion-based method, built on top of a pre-trained text-to-image model, that takes as input a portrait image of a person and a second reference image of the same individual, potentially captured under different conditions. The goal is to seamlessly replace the input face with the reference face, while keeping the background intact. Surprisingly, despite the clear real-world utility of this task, no recently published work has directly addressed face replacement in this specific setting. To support this goal, we construct a large dataset of image pairs depicting the same person under varying facial expressions and poses. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach produces more realistic and identity-consistent results than existing face reenactment models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) aims to perform old-category recognition and novel-category discovery in the same unlabeled set. Early methods address both objectives through clustering and evaluate the exchangeable predicted indices with global Hungarian (GH) matching. Recent methods adopt trainable parametric heads, using supervision for old-category recognition and discovery-oriented objectives for new categories. In this way, the output space becomes partially exchangeable: old indices are semantically anchored, while new indices remain exchangeable. However, these methods still apply global matching, which can reassign anchored old indices to other categories and hide some prediction errors. Empirically, we are surprised to observe that this reassignment can induce an overestimation of new-category accuracy by up to 25.63 points. We term this phenomenon semantic mismapping and further derive a permutation-cycle condition that characterizes when semantic mismapping can increase the global matching score. To fix this issue, we propose Semantics-Preserving Evaluation (SPE), which preserves the supervised semantics of old indices and restricts Hungarian matching to the new region. Furthermore, we propose Mismapping-Mitigating Optimization (MMO) to alleviate the prediction errors that can give rise to semantic mismapping, combining a region-balance loss (RBL) for old/new routing with a novel-diversity loss (NDL) that encourages orthogonality among new prototypes. Theoretically, we show that minimizing the two losses can tighten an upper bound on spurious accuracy gains from semantic mismapping, with controllable residual terms. Empirical results under both GH and SPE demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Few-shot semantic segmentation typically identifies target objects based on positive support examples of a single semantic class. In practice, however, positive cues alone are often underspecified: for instance, "red cars" may be confused with cars of all colors, "vehicles" may include cars that should be excluded, and "material-defined objects" may span multiple categories. Fulfilling precise requests requires positive exemplars to provide inclusion evidence and negative exemplars to act as intent-defining constraints for exclusion and disambiguation. We formulate this setting as Specified Visual Prompt Segmentation (SVPS), where the target concept is specified by both positive and negative visual prompts. In this work, visual exemplars refer to support-image regions specified by bounding-box prompts. To study SVPS, we introduce CASE (Concept-Aware Segmentation from Positive and Negative Visual Exemplars), a benchmark built on COCO and PACO. CASE encompasses six category- and attribute-level task families: exclusion, isolation, union, attribute exclusion, attribute isolation, and attribute union. We evaluate SAM 3 under this setting and find that, despite its powerful positive-only few-shot segmentation capability, frozen SAM 3 does not consistently treat negative visual prompts as exclusion evidence. To address this, we present Concept Prompt Adaptation (CPA), a conservative LoRA-based adaptation baseline that improves positive-negative prompt interpretation on CASE while remaining competitive under positive-only few-shot segmentation. Code is available at https://github.com/WongKinYiu/FSS-CASE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for real-time photorealistic novel view synthesis. However, its training-time densification remains largely heuristic, often resulting in redundant Gaussian proliferation and inaccurate geometric reconstruction. Existing methods predominantly rely on first-order screen-space gradients to trigger cloning and splitting, which fail to distinguish true geometric discontinuities from high-frequency textures or noisy residuals. To address this limitation, we propose ACD-GS, an Asymmetric Curvature-aware Densification framework for compact and geometry-aware 3D Gaussian optimization. Our key insight is that second-order depth responses provide a more reliable structural prior for density control, enabling asymmetric regulation of cloning and splitting to better capture geometric discontinuities. Building on this, we introduce a multi-view photometric consistency constraint to suppress transient single-view errors, together with a capacity-aware adaptive regulation strategy that balances densification and pruning during training. Unlike post-hoc compression or quantization-based approaches, ACD-GS directly prevents redundant Gaussian generation during training, yielding inherently compact representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method reduces nearly 60% of Gaussian count and storage while maintaining competitive rendering fidelity, consistently outperforming existing methods across multiple datasets. Our code is available in https://waveviewer.github.io/ACD-GS/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Quantifying degradation between a pristine signal and its degraded observation is fundamental to signal processing, yet existing full-reference measures are inherently modality-specific. We introduce SymKL, a unified full-reference degradation measure based on implicit neural representations (INRs). An INR is fitted once to the pristine reference and characterized by its diagonal Fisher information, which captures the local information geometry of the learned representation. Evaluating the Fisher information with the pristine reference and a degraded observation yields two parameter-sensitivity profiles whose symmetric KL divergence defines the degradation score. The proposed formulation requires only the reference and degraded observation, without knowledge of the corruption process or degradation severity, making it applicable across images, audio, and 3D data. We further derive an exact closed-form expression for the diagonal Fisher of SIREN, enabling efficient computation. Experiments on image, speech, and volumetric 3D benchmarks demonstrate that SymKL accurately tracks degradation severity, remains monotonic where conventional metrics saturate or fail, and provides a unified modality-agnostic measure of signal degradation. Code will be made publicly available after the review process.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Efficient object detection on resource-constrained edge devices is essential for safety-critical applications such as fire-smoke monitoring. However, current lightweight frameworks, such as YOLO26, still rely on the parameter-dense multi-branch Feature Pyramid Network (FPN), preventing full exploitation of their lightweight advantage. Replacing FPN with YOLOF's single-input-single-output (SiSo) neck is a promising solution but causes substantial accuracy loss. To alleviate this, we propose YOLOF-MLD, a CPU-efficient multi-level distillation (MLD) framework that combines a YOLO26n backbone, a SiSo neck, and MLD. It aligns features at the backbone via sliced-semantics matching, neck with receptive-field matching, and response levels. On D-Fire and Annotated Fire-Smoke (AFS) datasets, YOLOF-MLD reduces the parameter count of YOLO26n by 35.7\% and achieves a 62.3\% speedup on Raspberry Pi 5, attaining 71.6% and 59.9% mAP@50, respectively, compared with 75.5% and 60.0% for YOLO26n. These results demonstrate a competitive accuracy--latency trade-off for edge deployment. Our code is available at https://github.com/XiaolongWU111/YOLOF---MLD/blob/main/README.md.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate lung tumor segmentation in computed tomography (CT) is essential for treatment planning, response assessment, and quantitative imaging analysis. However, 3D lung tumor segmentation remains challenging because tumors occupy only a small portion of the whole volume and exhibit heterogeneous appearances across anatomical locations. In patch-based training and sliding-window inference for 3D medical image segmentation, location-dependent heterogeneity is further amplified because cropped image patches provide limited global anatomical context, making it difficult for a model to adapt its feature processing according to the anatomical region from which each patch is extracted. To address this limitation, we propose an Anatomical Prior-guided Routing Module (APRM) for lung tumor segmentation. For each image patch, we obtain anatomical prior information of regional occupancy and deterministically select an anatomical routing gate. The selected gate then performs sparse feature routing over a shared expert adapter pool, enabling anatomy-specific feature adaptation while sharing expert capacity across regions. We evaluate APRM on two datasets with various backbone networks and demonstrate consistent improvements over baseline models. Furthermore, feature visualizations show that APRM produces different feature responses depending on patch location, indicating that the proposed routing mechanism effectively adapts feature processing according to anatomical context.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal large language models can recognize visual content yet struggle to reason reliably over it across multiple steps. In counting, a model may see all objects but lose track of which ones it has counted. We argue that such failures, commonly attributed to perception, reveal a reference gap: the failure to bind language to the relevant visual entities, regions, or locations. Motivated by the theory of visual routines, we test whether external references such as numbering can narrow this gap. We introduce ReGap-Bench, a paired benchmark of five visual reasoning tasks in which the scene, question, and answer are fixed and only the reference varies. Results show that task-matched references improve accuracy by up to 25\%, while mismatched references provide little benefit or even hurt performance. These findings identify the reference gap as a bottleneck distinct from visual perception and show that constructing task-appropriate visual references can serve as a training-free visual skill for improving multimodal reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Formula-Driven Supervised Learning (FDSL) enables vision pre-training without real images, but existing methods cast pre-training as classification on discretized labels, discarding continuity in the generative process. We propose Formula-Driven Supervised Metric Learning (FDSML), which derives relational supervision from image-generation parameters beyond discrete class labels. FDSML combines FineFractalDB, our refined fractal dataset with higher intra-class variation and more stable structures, with a Parameter-Aware Triplet (PA-Triplet) Loss that assigns positives and negatives according to distances in the underlying Iterated Function System parameter space. This objective trains embeddings to respect parameter-derived relative similarity orderings. Experiments show that FDSML improves transferability to image retrieval while remaining competitive on downstream classification and improving robustness under several corruption settings. On ResNet-50, Recall@1 improves from 65.6% to 74.0% on Stanford Online Products and from 56.2% to 78.4% on Stanford Cars over FDSL pre-training. These results suggest that parameter-derived relational supervision provides a stronger synthetic pre-training signal than label-based classification for transfer beyond recognition.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical AI learns from the answers doctors record, but rarely from how they interpret the evidence to reach them. Here we introduce PathCowork, a framework that turns pathologists' narrated examinations into supervision for visual grounding and procedures that guide diagnostic AI agents. Synchronized gaze, narration and navigation link visual findings to the process of interpreting them. Across 48 sessions from 16 pathologists, the platform captured 1,028 minutes of slide review. Using these data, we trained PathCowork-1T for server deployment and PathCowork-27B for local deployment, with improved visual grounding. Across five organ-specific evaluations, agents combining this grounding with recorded diagnostic procedures achieved primary task scores averaging 17.9 and 24.1 percentage points higher than Claude Code and Codex, respectively, with shorter processing times and lower inference costs. Compared to the state-of-the-art multiple-instance learning AIs, the PathCowork agent-proposed regions achieved higher prostate-grading scores with limited slide labels. PathCowork can also help construct pathology AI benchmarks where questions derived from the recordings identified difficulties in recognizing viewing context and rejecting inapplicable premises. In summary, PathCowork proposes a new paradigm to scale up the data collection and train pathology AI through transparent reasoning from diagnostic sessions, opening a path towards systems that learn directly from routine clinical practice.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) enable powerful multi-image synthesis but incur substantial computation from long visual contexts. While token pruning offers a direct route to accelerating inference, existing methods are primarily adapted from understanding tasks, suffering from insufficient reference image exploration, confused conditional guidance, and pooling-induced ambiguity that degrades generation quality. To address these issues, we propose CATP, the first training-free context-compression method tailored to UMM generation, which \textbf{C}alibrates the discrepancies of general \textbf{A}ttention-based \textbf{T}oken \textbf{P}runing for better quality-efficiency trade-off. Concretely, building on independent layer-wise pruning during denoising, we introduce Guidance-Consistent Pruning (GCP) to synchronize pruning decisions across conditioning branches, and a Taylor-Calibrated Attention Metric (TCAM) that derives a theoretically grounded second-order correction for pooling-induced bias. In addition, a Fast Scoring Operator (FSO) fuses the calibration into tiled online Softmax to reduce pruning cost. Extensive experiments across representative UMMs and benchmarks demonstrate that CATP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art token-reduction methods, achieving up to 2.09$\times$ attention-core speedup.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Compositional motion transfer requires a model to extract motions from multiple reference videos, and apply each motion to a user-specified object in one target scene. Existing methods normally struggle to reliably assign different reference motions to the correct target objects, thus a motion intended for one object may affect another. Meanwhile, some motion-specific tuning approaches are still facing another issue: they must run a new optimization whenever the reference video changes, making repeated transfers inefficient. To address these limitations, we propose RART, a framework that unifies object-wise motion extraction, target-region anchoring, and frame-aligned injection into a frozen video Diffusion Transformer for multi-object motion transfer. For each reference motion, RART extracts temporally ordered features from the masked reference video, and then conditions them on the target first-frame mask to form mask-anchored motion tokens. Frame-aligned motion cross-attention allows each generated frame to read only the motion tokens at the corresponding time slots, composing all object motions in one denoising process. During training, a motion-attention routing loss encourages each target region to attend to its corresponding motion tokens and reduces interference between objects. Therefore, RART can handle novel reference-target combinations without per-video optimization. Experiments show that RART achieves accurate motion preservation, efficient inference, and strong compositional generalization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Intermediate-fusion cooperative perception lets a vehicle detect objects beyond its own field of view by fusing features shared by nearby agents. When only a collaborator's cameras are degraded by fog, motion blur or exposure errors, the features it shares drift away from the training distribution while the ego input stays clean, and cooperation can become worse than not cooperating at all. Test-time batch-normalization adaptation, the standard training-free remedy, estimates statistics over the pooled batch of all agents and therefore normalizes both the clean ego and the corrupted collaborator with a mixture of two distributions. We show that this mixing harms even the clean ego: when the ego shares BN statistics with a corrupted collaborator, its detection from its own features drops by 3.58 to 6.68 AP@0.5 points although its cameras are untouched. We propose AgentNorm, an agent-wise test-time normalization that i) estimates the normalization statistics of every collaborator from its own current observation and ii) anchors the ego to its source statistics, so that the ego path remains exactly the original model. AgentNorm has no learnable parameters, needs no gradients or labels, is independent of the order of test frames, and runs locally on each agent without changing the transmitted features. On camera-based OPV2V with four fusion checkpoints and collaborator-only corruptions, AgentNorm raises AP@0.5 under severity-5 fog from 29.42-37.25 to 43.32-58.86 and outperforms pooled normalization by 5.53-12.94 points under fog and motion blur, at an extra latency of 2.3 ms per frame. On the real-world DAIR-V2X-C dataset, it outperforms pooled normalization in all ten infrastructure-corruption settings. We also report the cases it does not fix: occlusion, which removes image content rather than shifting feature statistics, and the case in which all agents share the same shift.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study domain generalization for LIDAR semantic segmentation. Deep neural networks achieve strong performance on 3D point clouds, yet real-world LIDAR exhibits sensor-specific sampling patterns that hinder transfer across datasets. In many applications, performance on completely unseen sensors is crucial. A model should preferably work on a new setup without prior access to its data or labels. To this end, we introduce a source-only training scheme that adds an occupancy prediction auxiliary objective. We aggregate source-domain scans into a simple world model and use it as the supervision signal, replacing single-scan standard segmentation training.
  A key property of our approach is that it does not alter the input scan. During training and testing the network consumes the same single-frame LIDAR input, only the supervision signal is enriched via the occupancy objective. At deployment, there is no runtime overhead as the auxiliary head can be dropped for pure segmentation, or kept to obtain scene completion alongside semantic segmentation. In an experimental evaluation across distinct LIDAR datasets, we demonstrate that a model's generalization to new domains can be significantly affected by how it is supervised. In certain setups, performance on unseen domains improves without any explicit domain adaptation (no target scans, no target labels, no sensor priors) and without prediction refinement. At the same time, our method maintains competitive source-domain segmentation performance and incurs no additional inference cost.

</details>
